# Feature Log — 2026-10

Newest first. One entry per commit (or per logical change). Index and how to add an entry: `README.md` in this folder.

### 2026-10-24 — Mini-games XX: 🔨 Word Forge's Expert, review round 2 (#186)
Both reviews of the Expert's round 2 fixed. **No answer in sight:** three
of the wall's page names were answers ("Water words" by "What does
“aqua” mean in aquamarine?", "Many words", "Sun words") — now named by a
root ("Aqua words", "Poly and multi words", "Sol words", "Spect words"),
every page's mark a plain 📜 (☀️ was by “sol”); a word's card opened the
next word's page ("Meter is Greek for measure", micrometer next) — now
the next two words' families are shut there, and a shut page is marked 🔒
and says why ("pages open between words"); twelve meanings Dot says of a
wrong try gave the answer in other words ("Telemeter … measures" by
“meter”, "Induct … into" by “in”, parasol's "sunny") — reworded, and
`forge.test` holds every line to the asked meaning's every word. **A page
tapped comes into sight** (it opened under the fold at most sizes, every
band), a tap as it comes let be, and only the page read is said. **Hints
that point at one tile:** the asked root by as many first letters as tell
it from the tray's others ("so" beside sup, not "s" twice). **A root of
the same meaning in its tray** (poly and multi, aqua and aque: 1 word in
27 met one). The badges (🔒, New!) sit under Dot's bubble as the panel
scrolls; Expert's card doesn't repeat her cheer (her bubble's in sight
below it on every phone); the side panel's focus ring from the keyboard; multiport
and multifactor are words. TC-2613–2616.

### 2026-10-24 — Mini-games XX: 🔨 Word Forge's Expert, review round 1 (#186)
Both reviews of the Expert's round 1 fixed. **Every split the word's own:**
aquatic (aqu + -atic, not aqua + tic) and aquarium (aqu + -arium) are
replaced by aqueduct (aque + duct, "lead") and aquamarine (aqua + marine);
as is glossed "towards" (ad-); para's other meaning is said ("Greek para,
“beside”, is another: paragraph."). **The wall on Expert is Expert's:** its
pages taught the young bands' (-s, solar as a compound word) and put the
answer in sight ("Graph, “write”" as "graph" was asked); now each page has
a name without a meaning ("Graph words") and a note tying its three words,
and opens once the word's forged and its root named. **Every word in
turn:** the 27 come in a fixed order (they came back three rounds on, five
not till round 10). **Hints that teach:** a miss's hint never gives the
asked root's meaning (44% did), a part meaning the same as the right one
is said so ("“multi” means “many” too — but polygon takes the other one."),
a root with no other word has a clue, and Medium's add-on rule is gone.
Dot names the root when it's right ("“sol” means “sun”."), and the root
step's bubble no longer repeats the question. Multimeter and induct are
words; polygon's and multiply's meanings are true of every case; the
reward keeps page names with a comma apart. TC-2610–2612.

### 2026-10-24 — Mini-games XIX: 🐉 Ember Air Mail's Expert, review round 2 (#186)
Both reviews of the Expert's round 2 fixed. **The compass holds still on a
phone held sideways:** centred beside the words, it moved 65 px under the
thumb as they changed (at 844 × 390 a tap flew the wrong way); now it's
held at the row's top. **The globe gets a tablet's width:** held upright,
the side panel's under the board (702 px wide, was 402), and on a laptop
the globe's side panel is 300 px. **The labels say which side is west:**
bare longitudes on a phone say W and E by 0° ("30W 0° 30E"), the 180° ends
from 18 px. **The time:** a wrong time past midnight says the day ("23:00
— the evening before", against Tokyo's 09:00 in London); each round has a
city whose clock is the line short of it (Caracas and Jakarta for Los
Angeles and Bangkok — 12 of 15 were the line beyond, so "the line beyond"
won); on a short screen why 15° is an hour is said by Pidge, so a wrong
time's words fit; "No timer." A coordinate is kept on its line in the
clue, the ticket, the time card and the fact; the longitudes sit on their
lines inside the map's frame; the arrow keys mentioned only while flying.
TC-2603–2605.

### 2026-10-24 — Mini-games XVIII: 📜 Index's Timeline's Expert, review round 2 (#186)
Both reviews of the Expert's round 2 fixed. **The years asked fairly:** of
four answers, the right one was always the smaller of the only two a year
apart, so the sum never had to be done. Now six, in three pairs a year
apart — the answer and the year 0 counted, a hundred out and that with the
year 0, the years taken away and one more — in an order by the drawer's
scrambled seed (consecutive seeds put the answer last in every odd round's
first drawer), and the title card asks the years without giving the method.
**The line in sight as a drawer starts:** round 1's scroll measured the
words under the cards and, where the cards didn't fit, scrolled the line
off the top (Tricky too); now the cards' grid is measured, the line's top
is kept, and up to 600 px high a dated drawer's cards leave their pictures
off and say "c." (`timeline/layout.ts`'s `startScroll`, table-tested). **On
short screens the line's years are seen:** in the years step the heading's
a reader's and the six answers a row; filed, the lesson is three lines in
the bar and whole in Index's bubble; the scroll keeps the line clear of the
bar. Held upright, the two cards asked about are shown by the question.
Shorter wrong-count words; "400,000 BCE" inside its label; the ticks clear
of the names at 568 × 320; the book's names whole at 667 × 375; the line's
pictures off under 360 px on every band. TC-2599–2602.

### 2026-10-24 — Mini-games XIX: 🐉 Ember Air Mail's Expert, review round 1 (#186)
Both reviews of the Expert's round 1 fixed. **The globe gets the board:**
the post office scene and the town's map key were over and beside it — at
1024 × 768 it was 297 px wide, now 578. **The grid's read:** a move said
the square's lines, so a teen flew east till the words held 140° E; now it
says the way and the continent ("East. Ember's over Asia."), the lines for
a screen reader only, and on a phone the longitudes are numbered every 30°
(bare numbers), not 90°. **The time's a real question:** it gives the
city's longitude first ("Tokyo is at 140° E. It's 09:00 in London, in
January: what time is it there?") and says why 15° is an hour (the Earth
turns 360° in 24 hours); four times — the right one and the other line's,
and both the wrong way — so which way picks a pair and the nearest line
picks in it; a city's London hour moves on each time round the cities (each
was always asked at the same one). A 24-hour clock ("09:00"); a wrong
square across the equator or 0° says so; south of Australia is the Indian
Ocean; Tokyo "one of the biggest cities", Rio "River of January", New
York's subway "one of the biggest"; an arrow key flies at once; plain
"yet." and "That's the last one."; "Pacific" off the equator. TC-2595–2598.

### 2026-10-24 — Mini-games XX: 🔨 Word Forge's Expert (#186)
The tenth and last Expert (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §8.5): **etymology.**
Every family has three Expert words built from Latin and Greek roots —
solar, solstice, parasol; aquarium, aquatic, aquanaut; teleport, telegraph,
telepathy; geography, calligraphy, geology and more — with every root §8.5
names (port, dict, spect, aqua, chrono, geo, -logy, -meter), and a round
draws from all nine families. The tiles are the roots alone, no meanings;
Dot says the meaning in everyday words that never give away the root she'll
ask about. Forged, she asks what one of its roots means ("What does “sol”
mean in solar?"), from four — the root's, the other part's and two others,
in an order by the word. A wrong one is said (the other part's as the
other part's; another with a word the root's in to think of), marked tried,
and picked again. The card says how the word's built and more words with
the root. Every pair of roots a tray can make is a real word Dot explains
or a not-word, the rarest looked at. Dot plain; forged at the first hammer
and the root right first pick is the Expert seal, and the reward keeps the
first missed word's lesson. The meanings and Next wait for a tap's second,
not a key's. Every game has its Expert now. TC-2606–2609.

### 2026-10-24 — Mini-games XIX: 🐉 Ember Air Mail's Expert (#186)
The ninth Expert (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §8.5): **latitude and
longitude on a 15° grid, and time zones.** The world is drawn again in
squares of 15° — 24 across, 12 down — its lines numbered up the side and
along the bottom, the equator and the prime meridian stronger. The post
leaves London, and a parcel goes to a city by its coordinates ("A letter
for Tokyo, 36° N, 140° E."): its square is between the lines either side,
and Ember flies there eight ways, east and west joined. A wrong square says
its own lines and where the city's are, then which way. Delivered, Pidge
asks what time it is there when it's 9:00, 12:00 or 15:00 in London in
January — an hour for each 15° to the line nearest the city, ahead to the
east, behind to the west — from three: the right time, the hours the wrong
way, and the other line's. A wrong one says what's wrong with it. Fifteen
cities on five continents whose January clocks keep that line; the lesson
is its coordinates, its clock and a true thing about it. Pidge plain;
delivered first go and the time right first pick is the Expert seal, and
the reward keeps the first missed parcel's lesson; every stamp comes. The
times and Next wait for a tap's second, not a key's. TC-2591–2594.

### 2026-10-24 — Mini-games XVIII: 📜 Index's Timeline's Expert, review round 1 (#186)
Both reviews of the Expert's round 1 fixed. **The years' answer gave
itself away:** sorted, it was always the middle of three, and the third
(the years taken away) was often absurd. Now there are four — the answer,
the year 0 counted, a hundred out and the years taken away — in an order
by the drawer, a wrong one marked tried, and the two cards asked about
marked on the line; an "about" year is taken as it is ("Say candles came
in 500 BCE…"). **Every date starts in sight:** at 667 × 375 and 740 × 360
the washed-up cards' dates were under the Check bar, at 1024 × 540 and
640 × 505 a row under the board's foot, at 320 × 568 the card wanted next
under the bar. On short or narrow screens a dated drawer leaves its
pictures off and says "15th c.", a date's lines are balanced and tighter
there, and as a drawer starts the cards come into view clear of the bar —
in the page, or on a split screen the board. A clue says why the card goes
in the gap (across BCE and CE how they count, or the two dates), never a
century of a card not in the slip, and Index's young words stay off
Expert. A new teen's first drawer is Writing, its CE centuries the trap.
On the years a wrong count's words appear where they're seen (the answers
jumped 70 px). Two "what led to what" lines were untrue as said (the Burj
Khalifa stands on concrete walls; arc lamps came before the bulb). Every
drawer, on the title card, for the seal; Candles, Farming, Pyramid and
vaccine break at their syllables. TC-2588–2590.

### 2026-10-24 — Mini-games XVII: 🌳 Ringwood's Rings' Expert, review round 2 (#186)
Both reviews of the Expert's round 2 fixed. **Round 1's card covered the
beam:** on a laptop (1280 × 800, 1024 × 768, 1536 × 730) the step card
painted over the bottom of the strips, and a beam's thin rings — short
bars standing on its bottom — were gone (Tricky's core lost its edge too).
The strips are now as tall as they're drawn, the card scrolling under them,
with the slide buttons and the gold one in a row on every split screen;
what to do is a reader's there, Ringwood's bubble beside saying it. A
finished beam's strips give the lesson their room, and each step's card
starts at its top: at 640–740 × 360 the done card opened scrolled past its
heading, the next beam's where the last had been. The box under the
buttons keeps three lines' room on a short split screen too, so a wrong
year's shorter words don't scroll the years under the finger. As the beam
slides a reader hears where it is and the rain over its thin rings, not
all sixty words again; every ring stays the record's description. How to
read the year is in the ask (the step's tip never showed); Play says "Date
the beams" on Expert; the record's first year inside a phone's edge; the
dated beam's years on one line. Tests: the guide and the bark where
they're drawn; the nearest labelled year either way; the ends of a tap
along the record, the 21st said; Tricky's tip and tree count; the card from
its top. TC-2581–2583.

has it** (the real sky turns them up to 141°), and the job says what it

page's note leaves Vela's bubble at any tap. Tests: Expert's skies against
the sky rules and the names; the lessons' numbers from the catalogue; the
card's turn; an unsure tap that costs nothing; Orion's belt joined between
stars; the threshold; the padding box; the fainter lines and the ground.
TC-2570–2572.

### 2026-10-24 — Mini-games XVI: ✨ Vela's Star Charts' Expert, review round 1 (#186)
Both reviews of PR #110's round 1 fixed. **The sky is the real sky:** each

azimuths wrong; now a round's night is one of the coast's latitudes, and
each star is where it really is from there at its picture's time of night

the seal). The grid's lines are 3:1 and a pixel wide at every size, with
fainter lines every 5° on a 10° grid; its numbers 11–12 px beside a bigger
sky, and the sky as big as its room on a phone with them. Joins said
plainly; the first Tab never on the star to find; a star found keeps
focus into the joining; on the latitude a star tapped is named, not read,
and the answer's place moves; the lessons say what declination and the
celestial equator are, and the Southern Cross's by its own sum. Tests:
every star's coordinates the real sky's; the question's turns over every
page mask; the grid pinned, round north too; the latitude's words and
choices; Expert played with two misses, an unsure tap, the latitude's
taps; the waits on the latitude, Next and the chart. TC-2566–2569.
### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games XVIII: 📜 Index's Timeline's Expert (#186)
The eighth Expert (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §8.5): **centuries, years
across BCE and CE, and what led to what.** Every drawer deals all seven
cards, and a card gives its century where that's enough to place it ("5th
century BCE", "18th century") — else its year, where another card shares
its century, it's from the Stone Age, or it's a whole hundred CE (the
piano's about 1700). A clue gives a century's years ("the 18th century is
the years 1701 to 1800"). In order, the years come out and Index asks how
many lie from a BCE card to a CE card ("From candles (500 BCE) to gas
street lamps (1807): how many years?"), from three: the answer, with no
year 0, the year 0 counted, and the years taken away; a wrong one says
what's wrong with it, and the kid picks again. Each page says what led to
what among its cards — checked, and no wars, disasters or religion. Index
speaks plainly; in order at the first check and counted at the first pick
is the Expert seal, and the reward keeps the first missed drawer's lesson.
A card's date breaks at its spaces inside the card, and on a phone a
drawer with dates leaves the pictures off the line: seven in a row on a
short screen, or half a 320 px phone, "400,000 BCE" and "5th century BCE"
ran out of their cards (Tricky's years too). A wrong count's words come
into view. The years and Next wait for a tap's second, not a key's.
TC-2584–2587.

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games XVII: 🌳 Ringwood's Rings' Expert (#186)
The seventh Expert (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §8.5): **cross-dating with
climate data.** Each old beam comes measured — a bar a ring, as wide as it
grew — under the grove's rainfall record, a bar a year from 1995 with the
dry line at 500 mm. The kid slides the beam till its thin rings sit under
the dry years and its wide ones under the wet ("Not lined up: a thin ring
sits over a year with more than 500 mm of rain…"), then dates its last
ring, by the bark, from four years — its first ring's and a year either
side of the right one. The record has a tick a year and a label every
fifth; the beam's years are said only once it's dated, and the lesson
gives the rain in its thin rings' years. Ringwood speaks plainly. Every
beam lined up at the first check and dated at the first pick is the Expert
seal. On a split screen a beam's card — Tricky's too — is compact, so the
strips aren't under it (at 1280 × 800 it covered Tricky's beam in part),
and on a phone up to 640 px high Check starts in view (Tricky's started
under the fold at 320 × 568). A step's buttons wait for a tap's second,
not a key's. TC-2573–2576.

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games XVI: ✨ Vela's Star Charts' Expert (#186)
The sixth Expert (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §8.5): **sky coordinates.**
Every picture is on an altitude–azimuth grid — altitude up from the horizon,
azimuth round from north through east — as big as it is in the real sky (a
2° grid for Orion's Belt, 10° for the Big Dipper), the ground drawn where the
view dips under the horizon. Vela gives a bright star by its coordinates —
"Vega is at altitude 67°, azimuth 83°. Find it on the grid and tap it." — and
a wrong star says where it is and which way to look; then the picture's
joined from it. On the Little Dipper the North Star, due north, is as high
as tonight's latitude along the coast (20°, 15° or 25° N): the kid reads its
altitude and picks the latitude from four, the reason said either way. The
lessons are where each picture is in the sky: Mintaka on the celestial
equator, Vega overhead from 39° N, the Southern Cross only south of 27° N,
Cassiopeia and the Big Dipper never setting from the north, Regulus on the
ecliptic. Star names given, not shown; no hint for the coordinates; Vela
plain — after a miss "Joined, with a miss on the way.", not "That's it."
Every star found and every latitude read first time is the Expert seal. Right ascension on a chart would need the pictures redrawn from a
catalogue — not built (§8.5). Also, on every band: a step's buttons and
stars wait for a tap's second, not a key's. TC-2562–2565.

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games XV: 🎵 Echo's Call's Expert (#186)
The fifth Expert (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §8.5): **intervals, in the
tunes.** A round reads one tune from the staff, hears one and reads one
(five to seven notes, all five chimes); in each, Lyra marks two notes by
their place — "From note 1 to note 3: what interval?" — and the kid names it
from six: a minor or major 2nd, a minor or major 3rd, a perfect 4th or 5th.
Whichever is picked, she gives the method: "E down to C: 3 letter names, C D
E — a 3rd; 4 semitones — major." Then the tune's played back. Each tune asks
the intervals its own notes make, in turn round by round, and a perfect 5th
wherever it's there, so every kind comes up. On Expert the chimes keep
their letters but not their colours' names, and Lyra speaks plainly. Every
interval named at the first pick and every tune played with no wrong chime
is the Expert seal. An octave, keys and sharps and flats would need more
chimes — not built; §8.5 says so. Also, on every band: a step's buttons
wait for a tap's second, not a key's. TC-2550–2553.

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games XIV: 🪞 Mirror Mural's Expert, review round 1 (#186)
Both reviews of PR #108's round 1 fixed (code: 1 medium, 4 low, 2 nits +
test gaps; UX: 1 high, 5 medium, 5 low). **No other move paints the
picture:** every pattern is the same across and down, and five corner to
corner too, so as they came a half turn painted x = 3's picture, and two
mirrors a quarter turn's — the check couldn't tell a reflection from a
rotation, which is the lesson. On Expert the painted part's two colours now
swap in one place (the top-left quarter, one side of the diagonal, the top
row or the left column — the first that works) so that no other way of
copying it (any move, two mirrors, the square's turns and mirrors, a slide,
the quarter turns the other way) fits; tested for every pattern under every
move. **Step 1 is on a bare canvas:** the painted part's shape gave two of
the first round's three moves away (a triangle is y = x, a quarter the
quarter turns), so it shows only A and A′ till the move's named. **On a
phone** A and A′ were scrolled away above the moves: they're drawn small
beside the ask (on a phone on its side, the moves three across beside them
too), and Step 1 scrolls them into sight with That's the move; after a miss
the move's rule is over the canvas (it was only below the fold). **At 683 ×
600** the axes' room held the canvas at its tap floor, and Check the image
started under the footer: where the canvas is held so, the board scrolls
the gold button into sight as painting starts, the canvas's top kept
(every band). **A screen reader:** the kid's squares said the square they're the
image of, and its colour, before any miss; the painted part is read in the
arrow keys' grid now ("Square (2, 5) to (3, 6): yellow, given."), not
painted, and a square says what it's the image of once a check marks it.
**The words:** an expression stays on one line ("x = 3", "(6 − x, y)"
broke across lines on the buttons and the reward); "leave it bare where
that one is bare"; "as far on the other side"; "the perpendicular bisector
of the segment from each point to its image"; "the picture looks the same
after each quarter turn"; "The line, centre or vector shows…". Also: the
third round asks from the other A (it was the first round again); the
reward keeps the first missed picture's lesson; the letters are drawn over
the line; the free canvas draws x = 3; `PlayArea`'s `onClaim` takes the
game's own type; `stepScroll` is shared (`minigames/`); Expert's marks and
axes in `marks.tsx`. Tests: the quarter turns exactly clockwise; each
move's rule and lesson against `mapPoint`; the marks' and axes' places; a
wrong name alone and a check's miss alone each costing the seal; the move
and the canvas reset for each picture. TC-2544–2546.

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games XIV: 🪞 Mirror Mural's Expert (#186)
The fourth Expert (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §8.5): **transformations on a
coordinate grid.** On Expert the canvas has axes — 0 to 6 at its lines, x
across and y up from the bottom-left corner — and there's no paint to mix.
Step 1 names the move from a point and its image, marked on the canvas: "A
(1, 4) maps to A′ (5, 4)." The six moves are always the same buttons —
reflect in x = 3, in y = 3 or in y = x; rotate 180° or 90° clockwise about
(3, 3); translate by (3, 0) — and each takes A somewhere else; a wrong one
says where it takes A ("Reflect in y = x: A goes to (4, 1), not (5, 4).
Try another."). Then the kid paints the image ("Reflect the top-left
triangle in the line y = x.", "Rotate the top-left quarter 90°, 180° and
270° clockwise about (3, 3).") with each square said by its corners'
coordinates, and checks it. No line is drawn till a check finds a square to
fix: then the line, the centre or the vector shows and Madame Hue gives the
rule plainly ("A reflection in x = 3 takes (x, y) to (6 − x, y)."). The
lessons are the moves' (a half turn is a reflection in x = 3 then in y = 3;
four quarter turns, rotational symmetry of order 4). Every move named at the
first try and every image exact at the first check is the Expert seal —
and since the round's pay is claimed as the third picture matches, before
the free canvas, the stage's `onClaim` now takes the round's spot-on count
too, so leaving from the canvas keeps the seal. Also, on every band: a
step's buttons wait for a tap's second, not a key's (as the other games'
do). TC-2540–2543.

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games XIII: ⚙️ Clocktower Gears' Expert, review round 2 (#186)
Both reviews of PR #107's round 2 fixed (code: 1 medium, 1 low, 2 nits +
test gaps; UX: 1 high, 5 medium, 3 low + also noted). Most of it was round
1's own scroll. **On a phone on its side** (568 × 320) the job line stuck
to the page, so the step-start scroll took the board away — at each machine
and under a tap after a miss, whose second tap then pressed a crank gear or
Take all the gears off (every band). The scroll is now one rule
(`stepScroll`, pure and tested): Turn the crank wholly in sight and, on
Tricky and Expert, the job — or, under a stuck job line, the crank's gears
— not under what sticks at the top, moving no further than that takes;
never on a phone on its side, never on a peg tap. **The job line sticks on
Expert only** (Gentle and Medium at 501–600 px high had lost Tock's line
and the how-to under it), at the panel's very top (text showed in a strip
above it), and the panel's scroll padding is its height, so keyboard focus
isn't under it; Tricky's job line shows up to 800 px high too (its speed
and teeth are its job). What happened comes into sight with its button by
the same rule (the longer lesson left Next under the footer at 640 × 505). On Expert up to 600 px high and on a narrow phone it's
smaller and the four gears stand in a row, so Turn the crank isn't under
the footer at 640 × 505 and the numbers show at 320 × 568. **The lesson
bridges the ratio to the turns:** "The gear ratio, machine : crank, is 8 :
24 = 1 : 3: the machine turns 3 times for each turn of the crank, so 15
turns a minute became 45…". Also: Enter on the crank's peg straight after
Change the gears is a double Enter's second (it took the gear off); the
title card's gears turn opposite ways, their teeth alike; the frame leaves
the top and bottom rows' teeth room (they touched it, every band). Tests:
the scroll's rule; no scroll on a phone on its side or on a peg after a
miss; the double Enter; "Too much force"; the reward keeping the first
missed machine's working, or a clean round's last; Expert's empty and
not-joined words; the title card's pictures. TC-2536–2539.

**A peg after a miss:** the first peg tapped clears what

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games XIII: ⚙️ Clocktower Gears' Expert (#186)
The third Expert (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §8.5): **gear ratios in numbers.**
Tock's jobs on Expert are said in turns a minute — "Make the Great Hands turn
clockwise ↻, 45 times a minute. The crank turns 15 times a minute; the
machine's gear has 8 teeth." — or in turning force — "…with four times the
crank's turning force. Its gear has 32 teeth." — never as "twice as fast";
the kid works out the ratio, picks the crank's gear from four (8, 16, 24 and
now 32 teeth: each job made by exactly one, every size the answer somewhere),
and joins it up the right way round, as on Medium. A miss is said in the job's
numbers with the rule ("turned 30 times a minute, not 45. Turns out = turns in
× crank teeth ÷ machine teeth."); a right run gives the ratio and the trade
("24 teeth turning 8 is 3 : 1 — 15 turns a minute became 45, with a third of
the crank's turning force."). Every machine right at its first turn of the
crank is the Expert seal. Two pairs in a train (a compound gear) would need a
new kind of peg — not built; §8.5 says so. Also, on every band: a step's
buttons wait a moment for a tap as it starts (a double tap's second had
pressed Next machine or Change the gears before what happened was seen), as
the Bakery's and the Potion Lab's do; a key isn't held back. Tests:
TC-2528–2531.

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games XII: 🥧 Dot's Bakery's Expert (#186)
The second Expert (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §8.5): **ratios, rates and
percentage change, boxed in pie.** Dot's orders on Expert are never an amount —
"25% more pie than last week's 1 pie", "the bigger share of 1 pie, split
5 : 1", "enough for 4 people, when 1 pie feeds 6" — and the kid works out how
much pie that is, then cuts and boxes it as before (eight orders, each a
different amount, all on two pies; a test works each order's words out to its
amount). Less is handed over: always two pies on the board, so it doesn't say
whether the order is more than one; the ticket's tag is the order's numbers
("+25%", "5 : 1"), never its pie; a cut that can't make it is said after a
miss, not as it's picked. Dot speaks plainly — "Too little. Work out 25% of 1
pie, then add it on." — and a right box shows the working ("25% of 1 pie is a
quarter, so 25% more than 1 pie is one and a quarter pies."). Every box right
at its first close is the Expert seal. Also, on every band (the bench): the
step card comes first in a side panel up to 700 px high, with the order in
it — the cuts had started below the fold (all five even at 1366 × 650) — and
the cuts stack their word under their picture in the 300 px side panel
("Quarters" was cut to "Quarter"). Tests: TC-2517–2519.

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games XI: the Expert band, review round 1 (#186)

note says where the age lifted the band ("For your age, Tricky — your
Science level (5) alone suggests Medium."; it read as if the level picked
it). **The seal:** with the stars on the title card; on the reward, a new

clear the scene, by the round's jars (`jars-6/9/12` screens) — the 621 px
line had sent 1280 × 600 and 1024 × 600 to the side panel. Also: a Brew Book
page keeps a jar's pH back till it's brewed this round (it gave the answer);

key read once; the chooser two by two at 320 px; "In book" left off the

1024 × 600). Tests: TC-2510–2512.

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games XI: the Expert band, and 🧪 Potion Lab's (#186)
The games were a young kid's at every band: Tricky tops out at
upper-elementary content, and a new teen started on Medium. **A fourth band,
Expert** (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §8), for about 12–18: secondary-school
content in the same world, a plainer host, and an optional challenge with no
clock — the **Expert seal**, a round spot on every time (`mg:<game>:seal`;
shown on the title card and the reward, never paid, never needed for a star).
**The band suggested follows the age as well as the level:** a level is
relative to the kid's age, a band's content isn't — so it's the higher of the
level's band and the one for the kid's content age (age + (level − 5) / 2):
10–11 Tricky, 12 and up Expert where the game has it. A new 12-year-old is
offered Expert, not Medium; a 10-year-old at a typical level, Tricky; a young
kid's suggestion never drops. Built one game a PR: a game shows Expert once it
has it (`def.expert`), and one without plays Tricky if ever handed it
(`upToTricky`). **🧪 Potion Lab's Expert:** the pH scale in numbers, counted in
tens — Tadpole's order asks for "a thousand times as acidic as pure water",
the kid guesses the jar's pH, 0–14, and he says it plainly ("Spot on.", "One
step out — that's ten times."), with where it sits on the scale and what
neutralises it. Also: Potion Lab's counter, at 501–620 px high, ran its rows
of jars under the scene (640 × 505: the first row couldn't be tapped on any
band) — there the jars are in the step card, first in the side panel; the
order's key now reads after it with a space. Tests: TC-2505–2509.

**The wall:** a page read opened under the wall and Dot read it too, so the

("add -" / "es" split what's taught); the reward names the round's three words
and how each is built; "unless" and "autography" said so a kid can tell; the
footer's steps follow the first empty place; a part on the anvil says it can

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games X: 🔨 Word Forge, review round 1 (#186)
Both reviews of PR #104's round 1 fixed (code: 1 high, 4 medium, 6 low + nits
+ test gaps; UX: 3 high, 8 medium, 3 low + also noted). **Real words are
real:** Dot told kids "Unless isn't a word", "Suns isn't a word", "Photography
isn't a word" — every pair of parts a tray can make is now a real word she
names and explains ("Photography is a real word: taking pictures with a
camera. Not this one!"; "Scat … Try them the other way round!") or a not-word
on a list a test holds every pair to. **Hints build on what was right**
("“sun” first is right — the meaning tells you the rest"; "“flower” is in it —
but second!"), an add-on in the wrong place says so ("“-less” goes at the end
of a word"), -s or -es is always the kid's to pick (both are in the tray), and
Tricky's meanings are in everyday words, not the tiles' ("what a scientist
peers into to see germs"), so a root is worked out, not matched. **The
keyboard:** placing a part moved focus out of the game (14 keys a word) — now
on to the next part, then Hammer, a double Enter's second held back; a held
Enter presses once (`noRepeat`, now shared with the Timeline and Air Mail in
`features/minigames/keys.ts`); a tap starts on the tray, not a ringed part.
**What Dot says:** a double tap on Hammer after a wrong try kept the hint (it
said "Two parts on the anvil first"); what landed is said ("“sun” is on the
anvil"); the same words again are re-read; no message cut off on a phone,
Hammer above what she said so a long line never moves it (52 px at
1024 × 768), and each line shorter (no "Dot wants …" under the meaning). Also:
the ticket and the wall show a family's mark, not a part like the tray's ("un"
for kinder), and name the family only once its word's forged; a part back in
the tray fades in, no 24 px shake; "A new page on the word wall: Graph,
“write”!", marked New! and brought into sight; a page tapped again closes;
the forged word pops with sparks; Tricky's roots two rows on a short split
screen ("graphy" ran out of a 45 px tile), its bar one row there — the
meaning and Hammer, what Dot said in her bubble beside it; the steps along the footer each part and Hammer;
truer microphone, microwave and sunflower; each round starts at the next
family. Tests: TC-2497–2500.

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games X: 🔨 Word Forge (#186)
The tenth and last mini-game (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §5.10), stacked on
PR 9: **how words are built**, with Scrivener Dot at Inkwell Cove. Dot reads
out a meaning ("a tall flower that turns to face the sun"); the kid taps two
word parts from the tray — each goes on the anvil, in order (one on the anvil
tapped comes back) — and hammers them into a word. The real one meant goes on
Dot's word wall with its family. Gentle joins two words into one (sun +
flower, rain + bow) and makes more than one (cat + s, fox + es — "after s, x,
z, ch or sh, add -es"); Medium builds on a root word with parts at its front
and back (un-, re-, pre-; -ful, -less, -er, -est); Tricky joins Greek roots,
each tile saying what it means (tele "far" + scope "look"; photo "light" +
graph "write"). The other way round, a real word that means something else
("Telegraph is a real word — it means a way to send writing far away"), -s for
-es, or no word at all is each said kindly, with a hint. Three words a round,
each family a page of the wall (nine), each with a note on how its words are
built. Built on the first nine games' reviews: what Dot says is in the step's
card by the anvil (and, till she's said something, what to do), the tray
keeps each part's spot, the card beside the board held sideways, a bar kept
in sight under it on a short screen or a small phone held upright (bench:
Hammer was under the fold at 320 × 568, and the bar covered the tray at
568 × 320), a step's buttons wait a moment as it starts. Dot invites the kid
in a new line and — with no quest of her own — offers 🔨 as the thing to do;
the first round gives a Focus Tea. Tests: TC-2480–2484.

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games IX: 🐉 Ember Air Mail, review round 2 (#186)
Both reviews of PR #103's round 2 fixed (code: 1 high, 2 medium, 5 low + nits

across then down (the Arctic 6,000 km, the Pacific 15,000) — now it's from

Australia on the map" by the compass gave the answer before a try — now
"Which place fits the clue?", and a reader hears the place only once a
wrong delivery's named it. **Room on a phone held sideways:** the side panel
splits off from 768 px wide only with height (or from 1000 px), so 844 × 390
— most phones, for a game — gives the world's map 520 px, not 290 (a `wide`
screen); the post office scene is left out for the world and on a tablet
held upright (the world was 310 px at 1024 × 768). **Kinder at the edge:** a
soft bump and one small nudge, not a buzz and a shake; on the world "That's
the top of the world". Also: a stamp just stuck in stays in sight (the side
panel's scroll undid it) and is said in the card where the album can be out
of sight; a double tap on Deliver after a miss isn't a second miss; a held
Enter on an answer picks it once; a tap while a stamp's read is heard; a
round started with a key shows the map's frame; never a card too narrow for

nowhere else; the Southern Ocean's round Antarctica; the hint counts squares
as Ember flies (it counted diagonals on a four-way compass). Also: the

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games IX: 🐉 Ember Air Mail (#186)
The ninth mini-game (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §5.9), stacked on PR 8:
**maps and directions**, with Postmaster Pidge at Port Lantern's post
office. Pidge hands over a parcel and its clue; the kid steers Ember across
a map with the compass, one square at a time — "North-east! Ember's over
C4." — her route drawn as she flies, and delivers it. A delivery in the
wrong place says where Ember is and which way to go ("That's C4. The house
is 2 squares north and 1 west."); off the map's edge, a bump. Gentle flies
the bay with four directions, the place shown as a picture; Medium the
town with eight, its squares named like C5 (letter along, number up), a
house found by its square or by a landmark ("just north-east of the duck
pond"); Tricky a blocky map of the real world — continents and oceans, the
equator across it, its east and west edges joined ("Round the back of the
world!") — then "How far?" by the map's scale (1 square ≈ 3,000 km). Three
parcels a round; each kind of post is a stamp in Pidge's album (nine: the
compass, north at the top, the Sun's east and west; eight directions, grid
squares, map symbols; the continents, the oceans, the equator), and the
world's places each say a true thing. The map is sized to fit with the
step's card beside or under it, whichever lets it be bigger. Built on the
first eight games' reviews: a tap's wait as a step starts, the board back
in view, what Pidge says in the card too (on a short split screen, in her
bubble beside the board), the arrow keys fly Ember, a reader hears where
she is. Pidge has no quest, so 🐉 is her gold offer; the first round gives
a Hint Feather. Tests: TC-2470–2474.

### 2026-10-23 — Mini-games VIII: 📜 Index's Timeline, review round 1 (#186)
Both reviews of PR #102's round 1 fixed (code: 1 high, 4 medium, 5 low + nits
+ test gaps; UX: 3 high, 4 medium, 2 low + also noted). **Clues name only

readers:** focus starts on the step's heading (the first card lit up as if it

the others say "about 4,600 years ago" once filed. **Steady:** each card
leaves a slot its own size, and held sideways an empty place keeps a card's

check keeps its clue. Also: names lower-case mid-sentence ("after the
wheel"), "washed-up cards" everywhere (not "the tray"), the cards first for

smaller and clear of the names, Index's "the water mixed them up",
six facts made exact (the first LED light, the petrol car, a glider with a

### 2026-10-22 — Mini-games VIII: 📜 Index's Timeline (#186)
The eighth mini-game (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §5.8), stacked on PR 7:

things people built (no wars, disasters or religion) — and the kid taps them

on the timeline (one tapped on the line comes back), so every tap is in the

them. Then "Check the order": the right
ones stay, ticked; the others float back to their spots in the tray with a
clue for one ("A bulb needs electricity in homes — gas lamps lit streets
first."). Gentle has 3–4 cards far apart; Medium five across the centuries;
Tricky 6–7 with their years, BCE and CE ("in BCE, a bigger year is longer
ago."). The years come out on every card once a drawer's
filed. Three drawers a round, each a page of the timeline book (eight sets
of seven: light, travel, writing, flight, food, music, buildings, medicine),
each with a true thing about it. Built on the first seven games' reviews:
what Index says is in the step's card too, the board beside or under it by
how the screen's held, the page back to the board's top on a phone, a
step's buttons wait a moment as it starts, the tray never reflows. Index
invites the kid in a new line, and her 📜 shows quietly over her quest's
offer; the first round gives a Forget-Me-Knot. Tests: TC-2460–2464.

### 2026-10-22 — Mini-games VII: 🌳 Ringwood's Rings, review round 2 (#186)
Both reviews of PR #101's round 2 fixed (code: 2 medium, 4 low, 4 nits + test
gaps; UX: 2 high, 5 medium, 3 low + also noted). **Each step says what to
do:** a step's start keeps what was right and adds the next ask ("Four! The
last ring, right by the bark — … Now tap the widest ring — its wettest
year!"; "Yes — 2016: … Now find every thin ring: two dry years!") — on a short
screen nothing else said it, and a reader heard nothing new. **The slice
draws what grew:** each ring its width from the pith (a dot) out — the first
took in the middle and looked the widest, so a kid asked for the widest
tapped it; the bark is edged in ink and flecked, so it isn't counted, and the
words say "the last ring, right by the bark" and "the ring by the bark".
**Taps:** the wettest year's step wants its wet rings (a medium ring by the
widest took a third of it); the next to count reaches outward only, not back
into the ring just counted; each hidden ring button sits where a press is
that ring (the first's was the second's); the shared `useLiftTap` ignores a
right-click, drops a mouse's press when it leaves the board, and reads a
click's own pointer type (a reader's click was swallowed after some
presses). **Short split screens:** Tricky's two cores take the board's width,
its card compact under them (beside a card they were 3.5 px a ring), the
board 400 px for them so Ringwood's bubble has room, and a long word
wraps in any host's bubble. Also: no focus ring for a tap player at the
start (it carried over from the title card); the box by the wood gives a tip
rather than his words again; a core's years don't run together (the year
under, the dry years over); the bark at the core's end; a taller core on an
upright tablet; Tricky's heading is the beam's year ("Cut in 2003!"), its
label row spaced, its name going with the beam, the beam never past the
picture's end, a new tree's beam not sliding back; Slide waits as the step
starts; "one to go" in words; the ticket's chip at the right when it wraps.
Tests: the hit test as a table (`hit.test`), `useLiftTap.test`, each ring's
button against its ring, a tap and its click on the slice, the beam at both
ends, double taps on Medium, Tricky and Next. TC-2459, TC-2490–2492.

### 2026-10-22 — Mini-games VII: 🌳 Ringwood's Rings, review round 1 (#186)
Both reviews of PR #101's round 1 fixed (code: 1 high, 6 medium, 5 low + nits
+ test gaps; UX: 3 high, 7 medium + also noted). **A tap is a lift:** the
slice and the core acted on the press, so a swipe to scroll the page counted
rings, picked the wettest year, or buzzed — now the shared `useLiftTap`
(from Star Charts' fix) taps as the finger lifts, and a press that moves or
is cancelled taps nothing. **Thin rings are easy to hit:** a tap was on the
ring it landed in, so a thin ring (7 px on a phone) was as thin to hit as
drawn and a near miss said "wrong" — now the ring the step wants (the next to
count, a dry year not found) is 32 px wide to hit and any ring 24 px, the
thinner winning (`ringAt`); the core's rings are measured inside its border
(a thin ring's edge answered for its neighbour); the pith is a dot, the first
ring's, not a ring of its own. **Tricky makes sense:** its core is a living
tree's (it said "the oak that fell in the great storm, living today"), "Not
yet" says thin under thin and wide under wide (some wrong places line up
every thin ring), a tap along the tree's core moves the beam there (a fit
took up to 19 presses), the two cores are one picture (the beam was drawn
4 px off), a reader hears the rings as the beam slides, and its step chips
are slide, check, page. **The same tree, the same rings:** a young tree's
slice has its age in rings every round (the birch was 4 one round, 6 the
next), an event's year is the same in every tree (the bridge was 2019, then
2016), the sequoia's fire scar shows. Also: two widest rings both count and
both are named; the done bubble keeps why it's right, then the cheer; the
box by the wood says what Ringwood asked till he says more, and on a short
screen it's what to do; a core's card is always under it, at the top of its
room (beside a card its rings were 8 px a year, and it moved at the payoff),
and beside a slice or Tricky's cores on a short screen the card is narrower
(a core's 30 rings were 3 px each) — Tricky's wider once matched; the
tree's number by the heading on a short screen; the core's years drawn
whole; a found dry year gold; the next tree starts at its first ring; a tab
into the slice glows, a tap doesn't; "medium" for "middling", words for
numbers, "Do they match?" named; the beam's thin rings darker; the shared
ticket's chip by its heading (its words were a strip in a 300 px panel).
Tests: TC-2455–2458.

### 2026-10-22 — Mini-games VII: 🌳 Ringwood's Rings (#186)
The seventh mini-game (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §5.7), stacked on PR
#100: **reading tree rings**, with Old Ringwood in Eldergrove — the tree who
loses count of his own. A tree grows a ring a year, wide in a wet year and
thin in a dry one, and every tree in the grove shares the weather
(`weather`: the grove's dry and wet years, spaced unevenly). Gentle counts a
young tree's slice ring by ring from the middle out ("Four! That's the bark —
four rings, so the tree was four years old."; out of order, the next ring is
outlined) and then taps its widest ring, the wettest year; Medium reads a
core, middle to bark — the ring from the year something happened, counted
back from the bark ("…each ring in is a year earlier"), then every thin ring,
a dry year — and a fire's scar shows on the pine's and the sequoia's;
Tricky cross-dates, as scientists do: it slides an old beam's core along a
living tree's till the thin rings line up (they fit in one place only), and
so finds the year the beam's tree was cut. Three trees a round, each a page
of the grove book (eight: birch, willow, beech, rowan, oak, pine, yew,
sequoia), each with a true thing about it. A tap anywhere near a ring is
that ring (thin ones are hard to hit); from the keyboard one ring is in the
tab order and the arrow keys go in and out, each said by where it is and
how it grew; the beam's and the tree's rings are said for a reader as the
beam slides. Built on the first six games' reviews: what Ringwood says is in
the step's card by the wood (and his cheer, on a phone), the card beside the
wood on a screen held sideways from 560 px, the page back to the board's top
at each step on a phone, the card's parts never squeezed, a step's buttons
wait a moment as it starts, headings keep their line height; on an
upright tablet the card keeps one height through a tree, so the wood doesn't
move at the payoff, and on a split screen "…is in the grove book!" is said
to a reader only (the book's in sight). Ringwood
invites the kid in a new line and — with no quest of his own — offers 🌳 as
the thing to do; the first round gives a Lucky Clover. Tests: TC-2450–2454.

Both reviews of PR #100's round 1 fixed (code: 5 medium, 4 low, 1 nit + test

the sky (a decoy star's ring looked like "tap this one"); the star focus

### 2026-10-22 — Mini-games VI: ✨ Vela's Star Charts (#186)
The sixth mini-game (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §5.6), stacked on PR #99:

each card saying how long the light took ("The light you see from Alnilam

it (the stars are small to see but easy to hit); the arrow keys move to the
nearest star that way, one star in the tab order, each said by where it is
and, once joined, its name. Built on the first five games' reviews: pictures
not on the chart first, focus where each step starts, a step's buttons wait
a moment as it starts, a held key presses once, the scene is decorative, the
chart's heading wraps; what Vela says is under the sky too (her bubble can

comes into view whole, up to 700 px high the sky has the whole column
(under the scene it was a small square), a tap reaches at least 22 px on a
small sky, and the headings keep their line height. Vela invites the kid in a new line, and — with no
quest of her own — offers ✨ as the thing to do; the first round gives a

### 2026-10-22 — Mini-games V: 🎵 Echo's Call, review round 2 (#186)
Both reviews of PR #99's round 2 fixed (code: 1 low + test gaps from an

own row it pushed Next below the fold on a phone on its side). **Which way

the answer says which way the chimes went ("ends on C — a longer chime,

tapped then says to tap the staff; the note to play next is outlined and a
miss rings it red and shakes it, as the slots do; each played letter sits in
a strip under the lines (it covered C). **Gentle's five of eight:** the band
note says so, and Lyra says when a kid knows them all ("try Medium for three
more!"). **A tune echoed** shows her cheer in the card too (on a phone; on a split
screen her bubble's beside it, and the Music House's row is a little shorter,
24%, so the result card fits), and a light runs up the chimes (still with motion turned down); its jingle waits for the last
note, and a miss no longer buzzes over the chime it's compared with. Also:
the big buttons grow for two lines; a shaken slot keeps its shake as she
passes it; empty slots are clearer; a right note is counted ("C — yes! 2 of
7.", so two Cs are each said); the card asks for the sound; the step
headings keep their line height; the chimes' column is wider on a wide short
screen. Tests for every gap: a too-high miss, Tricky's third tune, the staff
drawn where it's said (`staffY`, `STAFF_LINES` in `echo.ts`), the marker, the
page scroll, the waits, the bell's shape and the older iPhone's audio. Tests:
TC-2420–2423.

### 2026-10-22 — Mini-games V: 🎵 Echo's Call, review round 1 (#186)

was below the fold — a wrong chime looked like nothing). **Listen is one
button:** "▶ Listen" becomes "↻ Listen again" in place and focus stays on it

room kept from the start, and waits a moment as it first appears — tapping
Listen again and again no longer goes on and answers. **A wrong-note tune is

the right note"), and "Hear it again" plays it as written. **The staff is one
staff:** five lines drawn once, right across, a treble clef, the note Lyra's
on ringed in ink with a ▼; picking a note while she plays stops her; the spot
step starts on the staff's first note. **A wrong chime shakes its slot only**

whole tune!" covered it), says "Listen…" while she plays, and asks for the

note in the tap. **Fit:** any screen on its side from 560 px puts the step
beside the chimes (mid-size windows stacked them, and Next fell below the
fold) and one held upright stacks them — a split one too, where side by side

the staff and Listen are shorter and the switch moves into the card; each

Next). Also: the first note comes after 700 ms
(time to look at the chimes); answering mid-tune stops her; played slots
light as she passes them; arrow keys move along the chimes; the staff's
answer says places, not note names; a miss says "a shorter chime, further
right"; the Songbook's notes carry their letters and wrap at 200% zoom;

"interrupted" context and peaks at 0.25. Tests: TC-2416–2419.

### 2026-10-22 — Mini-games V: 🎵 Echo's Call (#186)
The fifth mini-game (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §5.5), stacked on PR #98:
**pitch, melody and reading notes**, with Bard Lyra in Chromaria's Music
House. Lyra plays the start of an old tune on five coloured chimes — C D E F
G, red to blue, long to short — and each lights up as it rings; the kid says
whether it ended higher, lower or the same as it started ("Not quite: it
starts on E and ends on C, lower down. Lower!"), then plays it back, a wrong
chime saying where the right one is ("That was E. The second note is lower —
try another chime."). Gentle has three chimes and 2–4 notes; Medium five and
4–6; Tricky reads the tune from a staff, or listens for the one note Lyra
plays wrong and finds it on the staff, with 5–7. Three tunes a round, each a
note longer, each a page of the Songbook: eight public-domain openings (Hot
Cross Buns, Mary Had a Little Lamb, Frère Jacques, Au Clair de la Lune, Go
Tell Aunt Rhody, Ode to Joy, Old MacDonald, Lightly Row), each with a true
thing about it. The chimes ring through a small Web Audio bell
(`lib/tones.ts`) that follows the sound-effects setting — off by default, so
everything is shown too, and a "Chime sounds" switch turns it on; the stage
plays no music over them. Built on the first four games' reviews: tunes not
in the Songbook first, focus where each step starts, the step card under the
chimes on a phone (it growing never moves a chime), a step's buttons wait a
moment as it starts, a held key rings once, two columns only where they fit
(a `split` screen). Lyra invites the kid in a new line and offers 🎵 quietly
over her quest; the first round gives a Rainbow Ward. Tests: TC-2410–2415.

Both reviews of PR #98's round 2 fixed (code: 1 high, 2 medium, 2 low + test

square (it was under the mark); the mark and its ! fit inside small squares,
the letter showing; Madame Hue counts the marks down as they're fixed, then
"All fixed? Hold up the mirror again, darling!". Also: Tricky's opposite
pictures and Medium's green-and-blue avoid colours a colour-blind eye mixes

where the mix step fits under its heading; counts in words up to twenty-seven;
the reward shows the picture hung; Madame Hue's bubble stays in sight on a
tall screen; Gallery thumbnails tell cream from bare wall; the free canvas's
picture and the frames have their own names; the tests click squares by

### 2026-10-22 — Mini-games IV: 🪞 Mirror Mural (#186)
The fourth mini-game (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §5.4), stacked on PR #97:
**symmetry and colour mixing**, with Madame Hue in Chromaria's Grand
Gallery. Half of each picture is painted; the kid mixes the paint it needs
from two pots (red and blue make purple — a wrong pair is said, never
scolded: "Red and yellow make orange — the picture needs purple."), paints
the rest by tapping or dragging across the squares (or the arrow keys and
Enter), and holds up the mirror, which marks any square that doesn't match
yet. Gentle mirrors across one line; Medium across or down, with orange,
green and purple; Tricky four ways (two mirror lines), a half turn, or each
colour as its opposite, and mixes tints with white. Three pictures a round —
nine in the Gallery, each a true thing about symmetry or colour — then a
free canvas: the kid paints the left half, the mirror the rest, and "Hang it
in the Gallery" keeps it in their save (one flag whose name holds the
picture; a new one takes the old one's place). Rules pure in
`lib/minigames/mural.ts`; the screen in `features/minigames/mural/`. Built on
the first three games' review rounds: pictures not in the Gallery first; a
visit picks up where the last left off; focus where each step starts; the
canvas, its paints and the mirror together, so nothing scrolls under a
finger (beside the canvas on a phone on its side); results heard with what
happened. `useFitWidth` is now shared (`features/minigames/useFitWidth.ts`).
Madame Hue invites the kid in a new line and offers 🪞 quietly over her
quest; the first round gives a Mirror Charm. Tests: TC-2398–2401.

### 2026-10-22 — Mini-games III: ⚙️ Clocktower Gears (#186)
The third mini-game (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §5.3), stacked on PR #96:
**gear trains**, with Tock in Gearfall's Clocktower. Three of its machines
have stopped each round (the Great Hands, the Hour Bell, the Cuckoo Door,
the Weathervane, the Moon Dial, the Music Drum, the Chain Lift, the Star
Wheel, the Mill Sails — the Clocktower Machines, each page a true thing
about real machines). The kid taps pegs to put gears on them between the
crank and the machine, and turns the crank: every gear it reaches spins,
each the other way from the one turning it (arrows show which way; reduced
motion keeps them still). Gentle joins them; Medium needs the machine to
turn the right way — count the gears between, odd keeps the crank's way —
and three gears that all touch jam ("Clunk — jammed!", the two shaking
gears marked); Tricky picks the crank's gear (8, 16 or 24 teeth) for the
speed too: only it and the machine's gear set how fast, the gears between
change only the way. A run that isn't right shows what turned and says why;
nothing is lost. Rules pure in `lib/minigames/gears.ts` (a hex grid of
pegs, `run`, `check`, `solve` proving every job can be done); the screen in
`features/minigames/gears/` (each peg an SVG button by row and place, with
a drawn focus ring). Built on PR 2's two review rounds from the start:
machines not in the book come first, the result heard with what happened,
the board sticky at the top of a phone and beside the steps on a short
screen, nothing scrolled on a tap that moves the board, the room above the
board (the great clock, today's machine stopped or running) from `sm` up.
Tock invites the kid in a new line and offers ⚙️ quietly over his quest;
the first round gives a Spark Cell. Tests: TC-2388–2391.

### 2026-10-22 — Mini-games II: 🥧 Dot's Bakery (#186)
The second mini-game (`docs/DESIGN-MINIGAMES.md` §5.2), on a branch stacked on
PR #95's: **fractions with pie**, with Baker Dot in Lumina Village. A morning
is three orders from the village's regulars (Grandmother Wick, Keeper Sol,
Mayor Marigold, Guard Rook, Nib, Pip, Bramble, Innkeeper Poppy — each a real
NPC with a favourite pie, the Regulars' Book, a page the first time each is
served right). The kid picks how many equal slices to cut the pies into,
taps slices into the box on the pie (or − / +, the box said in words as it
fills) and closes it: any cut that makes the amount is right (two quarters
is a half), a box that's off is said back ("A little more — the order is
three quarters of a pie.") and fixed, a cut that can't make it says so.
Gentle cuts halves and quarters; Medium thirds, sixths and eighths and orders
more than a pie; Tricky asks in percentages and decimals and boxes two
people's orders together. The reward keeps the morning's "is the same as"
lesson; the first morning gives a Sunseed Snack. Rules pure in
`lib/minigames/bakery.ts`; the screen `features/minigames/bakery/` (`PieArt`
draws every pie; the scene's customer and Ember stand over the SVG as HTML,
not in a `foreignObject`). It's built on PR 1's two review rounds from the
start: focus at the first step described by the order, the order in the step
card on phones and short screens, each gold button scrolled into view, the
book read back under it, `focusRing`, one "🗺️ Map". **The host's button,
narrowed (#186 (g)):** Dot also gives a quest, and round 1's rule (never over
the host's quest talk) would have hidden her game until it was done — now the
game shows over a quest's offer and reminders without taking the quest, never
over its reward, and finishes only a step through the host. Dot says so in a
new line. The stage's title is a size smaller below 360 px wide (a longer
name). Tests: `bakery.test` (14), `DotsBakery.test` (6), `DialogueOverlay.test`
(+3), the registry's checks cover the new game. Rendered on the bench at six
sizes and in the real app (TC-2378).

### 2026-10-22 — A walk counts after five minutes, and two devices add up (#177, #179)
Two follow-ups from the progress history PR, the lead's calls. **A walk
counts (#177):** a day spent only walking the world sent nothing (no XP, no
round, no battle). Now once this device has counted `PLAY_DAY_MINUTES` (5)
active minutes for the kid on a day nothing has counted yet — the existing
minute rule: a game screen, the tab in view, input in the minute, the kid
playing — the minute watcher counts the day (`countDay` →
`profileStore.countMinutesPlayed` → `recordActivity`): the 🔥 streak, "last
played", 📈's week and the minutes agree, and the day goes up at once. Once a
day per kid (the record is marked played, shared by every window; each
window remembers its count), never on 👪 / the family screens, never another
kid's minutes; walking now keeps the streak too (#28). **Two devices add up
(#179):** new migration **0027** keeps each device's part of a day
(`play_day_parts`, no client access; the larger total per device, as 0026
merged a day) and works `play_days` out as their sum on every note — XP and
minutes added and capped, topics added with r ≤ a and the latest level,
noted if any part is, 16 topics. `note_device_play(kid, device, days)` is
the app's writer (0026's rules; a device id 1–64 of `[A-Za-z0-9-]`, `legacy`
reserved); `note_play_history` (same signature) writes the day's `legacy`
part, so a tab on the #89 build adds; a day's old values become its legacy
part the first time a part is written; two notes of one day take turns on
its row; parts are tidied at 35 days with the days and go with the kid. The
app makes a random device id per browser (`hazel-device-id`), sends with

before another way stays with `note_play_history` (never counted twice);
without 0027 it sends as before. The draft notice says each device's share

### 2026-10-22 — CLAUDE.md refactored: 9,061 lines → 150, the rest moved verbatim into docs that load on demand (docs only)
`CLAUDE.md` had grown to 9,061 lines (629 KB) — 7,528 of them the Feature Log, 1,165 the Architecture section — against Claude Code's documented guidance of under 200 lines per file (a long file "reduces adherence"). Nothing was dropped: every line but the three rewritten section headers (`## Architecture`, `## Error handling`, `## Conventions`, now the new files' titles) survives word for word, checked by diffing the old file's lines against the union of the new ones. **Where it went:** `docs/architecture/` (`decisions.md` = Stack + Decisions; `auth-and-family.md`; `state-and-flow.md`; `content.md`; `world.md`; `battle.md`; `typing.md`; `questions.md`; `backend-and-ci.md` = DB / CI / pull requests; `errors.md`; `conventions.md`; a `README.md` index), `docs/OPERATIONS.md` (the whole Commands block: benches, Tiled, asset sets, the recheck script, the 0024 / `delete-account` deploy order and auth settings, 0025, the review alerts) and `docs/feature-log/<YYYY-MM>.md` (the log split by each entry's month, order kept; `README.md` says how to add one). **The new `CLAUDE.md` (150 lines):** stack, commands, the decisions that hold, layout, the rules that apply everywhere (errors, single-file edge functions, the migration rules — so `build-apply-all.mjs`'s "CLAUDE.md → migration rules" still resolves — registry ids, strict TS, Tailwind's breakpoint gotcha, ModalLayer / PasswordInput / LazyBoundary, shuffled answers, the canvas and battle invariants, where tuning lives), a "read before you change it" map, the workflow (draft PRs, the review skills, the doc ritual) and its own size budget. A 10-line root `CLAUDE.md` points at it. **Path-scoped rules** (`.claude/rules/*.md`, `paths:` frontmatter, globs relative to the repo root): world, battle, auth-and-family, typing, content, state-and-flow, questions, supabase, ui-and-errors — each the area's non-negotiables plus the pointer to its architecture file, loaded only when a matching file is read or edited (nothing is `@`-imported: imports load at launch and would save no context). **The budget is enforced** by `.claude/hooks/check-claude-md.mjs` (target 150, warns at 180, fails at 200, and refuses a dated `###` entry in `CLAUDE.md`): a SessionStart hook prints the warning and the five-step trim recommendation into Claude's context, a PostToolUse hook on Edit|Write hands it over as `additionalContext` right after a `CLAUDE.md` edit, `.githooks/pre-commit` runs it when `CLAUDE.md` is staged (blocks at the cap), and CI's `test` job runs it after lint (a `::warning` annotation at 180, `::error` at 200). **The pre-commit ritual changed:** a commit under `src/` now needs this month's `docs/feature-log/YYYY-MM.md` staged (not `CLAUDE.md`), plus `ISSUES.md` and `TEST-CASES.md`; `CLAUDE.md` changes only when a rule, a decision or the map changes. READMEs, `PRODUCTION-DB-SETUP.md`, `ROADMAP-4X.md` and the digest comment in `generate-questions` point at the new places; migration comments ("see CLAUDE.md → migrations rules") were left alone — migrations are forward-only and the heading still exists. Tested: the checker's ok / warn (185 lines) / fail (201 lines, and a `###` entry) / `--hook session-start` / `--hook post-tool-use` (JSON on stdin, silent for other files) modes on a padded copy; the pre-commit hook blocks a `src/` change with no docs staged and passes with the three; `settings.json` parses, `ci.yml` parses. Left: #185. **Review round 1 (the same day, self-review):** the rules' globs named `x.ts` only, so a test file, the drift tests in `src/lib` (`questionScreen.test.ts`, `questionFlavors.test.ts`, `notify.test.ts`, `edgeImports.test.ts`), `audio.ts`, `spriteAnim.ts`, `App.tsx` and the two hooks loaded no rule — every glob is now `name*.ts` (the module and its test), braces are gone (plain YAML lists, no parser to trust), `useBattleQuestions*` goes with questions and `useInertOutside*` with the UI rule, and a script matched every `src/` file but the fixtures and assets against the globs; the supabase rule became `backend-and-ci.md` and also covers `ci.yml`, `vercel.json`, `scripts/`, `site/`, `package.json`, the hooks and `.claude/` itself (with the draft-PR, Vercel and "every rule gets a `paths:`" invariants); the pre-commit hook checks the **staged** copy (`--staged`, `git show :hazel-game/CLAUDE.md`) rather than the working tree, and skips the size check with a note when `node` isn't on the PATH instead of blocking with a misleading message; the root `CLAUDE.md` says to read `hazel-game/CLAUDE.md` before any task. The lib globs are extension-free (`lazyRetry*` takes the `.tsx` test too), and the first `--staged` fallback (`false ?? x` keeps `false`) crashed a plain run — caught by re-running every mode, fixed with a ternary. **Review round 2:** the PostToolUse hook matched only Edit | Write, but Claude edits files through Bash (`sed`, a heredoc) as often as not, so a `CLAUDE.md` change made that way raised no alert until the next session, commit or CI run — the matcher is now `Edit|Write|Bash` and the script also fires on a Bash command that names a `CLAUDE.md`; the README's migration range ("0001 → 0009") updated to 0025. **Review round 3 (the owner's call):** the root `skills/saas-code-review/SKILL.md` was a byte-identical copy of `.claude/skills/saas-code-review/SKILL.md`, in a folder Claude Code never reads and nothing referenced — removed; the `.claude/skills/` one stays. **Merged main (2026-10-22, PRs #89 and #90):** their additions to the old `CLAUDE.md` — the progress-history decisions, the 📈 Progress and `profileStore` text, the CI and 0026 notes, and seven Feature Log entries — were ported word for word into `docs/architecture/` (`decisions.md`, `auth-and-family.md`, `state-and-flow.md`, `backend-and-ci.md`), `docs/OPERATIONS.md` and this file (checked: all 596 added lines present); the ids collided, so this work's issue is #185 (main took #177) and its test cases TC-2312–2314 (main took TC-2111–2116).

### 2026-10-22 — Progress history follow-ups review: an old build's day keeps "XP not recorded", each step put on once across tabs, a read timed whole and given longer, a start's age from its own day, the limits from one place (#180, #181, #182, #183, #184)
`/saas-code-review` of the follow-ups commit (1 medium, 6 low, an open
question and an also-noted), with the lead's calls, all fixed. **An old
build's day (#181, medium):** a day `main`'s build left unsent was folded
in and sent to `note_play_history`, which marked it `noted_play` — 📈 read
"0 XP" for a day the kid earned XP on. Now a day is marked only when sent
with `noted: true` (0026: `noted_play = noted_play or noted`); the app
leaves it off a folded day (`bare`, kept on the device), and keeps it off
once this device adds play to that day — the old build's XP is still
missing; 📈 reads `noted_play` false as "XP not recorded" even when the day
has XP (it read any XP as noted). **Two tabs (#180):** one up-step kept beside the copy was put on
by both tabs of a kid (math 7 → 9). Every tab now adds its steps to what's
kept, and the first to read the row takes them all under the kid's Web
Locks lock (or, without the API, read-and-remove in one synchronous step);
a tab that finds its steps taken takes each topic it stepped from the copy
that tab left, so a stale read never undoes a step down. The also-noted:
XP earned unread is kept the same way, so a reload no longer loses it to
"the higher wins" when another device moved the row's XP. **The read
(#184):** the whole read races a timer — supabase-js's session wait
included, which the fetch's abort never reached — and each failed read
gives the next twice as long (10 s, 20 s, then 30 s), so a slow link that
does answer gets its row. **A start's age (#183):** starts carry the day
they were noted (`on`, set by `setSkillLevel`, kept by `normalizeSave`,
the profile readers and the merge); 0026's trigger works the age out from
it and the row's birth date as each start arrives and on a correction, so
a month corrected is exact and a start reaching the row late (the save's
copy, another device, played unread, a device with a stale birth date)
lands right; `topic_start_day` takes only a real day from 2020 to a day
ahead. Starts without `on` keep today's rule (Architecture says what's
exact and what's approximate). A grown-up's Save sends the birth date only
where the form changed it (`updateKid`, the open question). **Limits
(#182):** 📈's reader takes `MAX_XP` / `MAX_MINUTES` / `MAX_ANSWERS` and the
level constants; the start readers `MIN_START_AGE` / `MAX_PLAYER_AGE`
(new `MIN_START_AGE`); `playDays.limits.test` pins both readers (`?raw`, no
bare numbers) and the trigger's limits. 0026 changed in place (not yet
applied anywhere), still re-runnable; bundle rebuilt. Tests 2470 → 2494, the
full suite green; lint, `tsc -b`, `build:web` with the
placeholders (`ProgressPage` 39.76 KB, 13.06 KB gz); CI's migrations job
locally (26 migrations, the bundle twice, 20 SQL tests); 20 of 20 SQL and
41 of 41 client mutations caught. No screen changed. Deploy: as the PR
(re-run `apply_all_migrations.sql`).

### 2026-10-22 — Progress history: a down-step that survives a reload, a real 0 XP day, limits pinned to 0026, a corrected birth date's starts, a read that times out (#180, #181, #182, #183, #184)
Five follow-ups on PR #89, from the issues its review rounds logged.
**A down-step after a reload (#180 b):** a level step played while the
profile row couldn't be read was kept only in memory; the copy on the
device holds the levels played to, and a load merges it with the row by
the higher — so a reload (or Switch player) before the row was read lost
a step down. The steps are now kept beside the copy
(`hazel-profile-steps-<kid>`, `lib/profile`'s `UnreadSteps`); a load takes
the copy back to where it stood (`withoutSteps`) and puts the steps on the
row's levels once read (`takeUp`, shared with `retryRead`), then clears
them; they also go with the copy (`forgetLocalProfile`). (a), a step from
a made-up start carried onto the real level, stays — by design.
**A real 0 XP day (#181):** `play_days.noted_play` (0026), set by
`note_play_history` on every day it writes and never by `note_play_days`;
📈 reads it, so a 30-second typing day is "0 XP", "under a minute", no
answers — recorded and counted whole — while a day noted without its play
still says "XP not recorded". **Limits pinned (#182):**
`playDays.limits.test` reads 0026 (`?raw`) and holds every limit
`lib/playDays` repeats to it (window, days a call, XP, minutes, topic ids,
topics, answers, levels — now exported, the window as `DAYS_BACK` /
`DAYS_AHEAD`). **A corrected birth date (#183, the lead's call):** keep-first
stays; a grown-up's birth-date change (a 👪-gated column) moves each topic
start's age by the change in the kid's age today (`kid_age_on`, the SQL twin
of `calcAge` — `age.test` checks both against one table), within 3–18, and
keeps its level; nothing else moves an age. Levels aren't reset: the
family's cached copies merge levels by the higher and starts by union, so a
server reset would be undone by the next device that loads. The save's
`topicStarts` copy isn't moved (📈 reads the profile's first). **A read
that hangs (#184):** the profile read is called off after 10 s
(`READ_TIMEOUT_MS`, an `AbortController` on `.abortSignal`) and counts as a
failed read, so the retry and its back-off carry on. 0026 changed in place
(not yet applied anywhere), still re-runnable; bundle rebuilt. Tests 2450 →
2470, the full suite green; lint, `tsc -b`, `build:web` with the
placeholders (`ProgressPage` 39.73 KB, 13.03 KB gz); CI's migrations job
locally (26 migrations, the bundle twice, 20 SQL tests); 14 of 14 SQL and
27 of 27 client mutations caught; 📈 rendered on the stubbed app at 320×568
and 1024×768. Deploy: as the PR (re-run `apply_all_migrations.sql`).

### 2026-10-22 — Progress history review round 2: play on a day played elsewhere goes up whenever it's noted, a minute is the playing kid's, "0026 missing" is asked again, the kid's own 7 days, one pair of words, "At least …" (#169, #170, #172, #173, #174, #177)
`/saas-code-review` (1 medium, 3 low + open questions) and `/saas-ux-review`
(3 medium, 5 low + also noted) of PR #89, with the lead's calls, all fixed.
**A day played elsewhere (code, medium):** round 1 marked only the records

— spells and Pair Attacks (3 up), the Spire's top floors and a speed
